# `jarvis-client` — every source file (part 1 of 2)

Commit `35fd97c1bdd20b544fb3f63f66fff5e7ccea1413` on `docs/gemini-audit-bundle-2026-09-20`.

Generated by `tools/gen_source_bundle.py`, not written by hand. The previous
version of this file was hand-written and went stale without saying so — it
described this module as "step 0, no networking" long after it had grown a
network layer, an audio stack and a voice loop.

Contains the whole `jarvis-client` module, plus the CI workflows that build it,
because how a thing is tested is part of judging whether it works.

**This is part 1 of 2.** The module was too large for one upload, so it is split across files by whole file — nothing here is cut mid-file. Part 2 of 2 continues the same commit; the manifest below lists every file across all parts and says which part each one is actually in, so this document alone tells you what you are and are not looking at.

**46 files in this document, 108 total across all parts.**

Deliberately omitted, listed so their absence is visible rather than silent:

- `jarvis-client/app/src/main/res/mipmap-hdpi/ic_launcher_foreground.png` — binary image
- `jarvis-client/app/src/main/res/mipmap-hdpi/ic_launcher_monochrome.png` — binary image
- `jarvis-client/app/src/main/res/mipmap-mdpi/ic_launcher_foreground.png` — binary image
- `jarvis-client/app/src/main/res/mipmap-mdpi/ic_launcher_monochrome.png` — binary image
- `jarvis-client/app/src/main/res/mipmap-xhdpi/ic_launcher_foreground.png` — binary image

## `.github/workflows/android-apk.yml`

````yaml
name: Build Android APK

# Builds jarvis-android, and only jarvis-android.
#
# This used to be a generic detector that worked out whether the repo held a
# Gradle, Flutter, React Native or Capacitor project. That was right when the
# repo might contain any of them; it is wrong now that it holds two Gradle roots,
# because the detector picked one with `find … | head -n1` — readdir order, not a
# choice. It returned jarvis-client in a local checkout and jarvis-android on the
# runner, so which app the "epic-jarvis-debug-apk" artifact contained was decided
# by the filesystem. The path filter below says what this workflow is for; the
# working directory now says the same thing.

on:
  workflow_dispatch:
    inputs:
      java_version:
        description: "JDK version used for the Gradle build"
        default: "17"
  push:
    branches: ["**"]
    # The desktop endpoint has no bearing on the APK.
    # keystore/ is in here because the build now reads the shared debug key from
    # it. Rotating that key changes what every APK is signed with, and without
    # this the change would build nothing.
    paths:
      - "jarvis-android/**"
      - "keystore/**"
      - ".github/workflows/android-apk.yml"

# contents: write is for the release step at the end, which publishes the APK
# where a phone can actually reach it. Nothing else here writes anything.
permissions:
  contents: write

concurrency:
  group: android-apk-${{ github.ref }}
  cancel-in-progress: true

env:
  PROJECT_DIR: jarvis-android

jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@11d5960a326750d5838078e36cf38b85af677262 # v4
      - name: Put the signing key back
        # From the DEBUG_KEYSTORE_B64 repository secret, not from the repo. The
        # key used to be committed; it was public, so it was removed and
        # replaced. build.gradle.kts still reads keystore/debug.keystore from
        # the repository root, so that is where this writes it.
        #
        # github.workspace is explicit because jarvis-client.yml sets a
        # job-level working-directory of jarvis-client, and this file does not
        # belong there.
        working-directory: ${{ github.workspace }}
        env:
          KEY: ${{ secrets.DEBUG_KEYSTORE_B64 }}
        run: |
          if [ -z "$KEY" ]; then
            echo "::error::The DEBUG_KEYSTORE_B64 secret is missing. Stopping on purpose:"
            echo "::error::build.gradle.kts guards the keystore on exists(), so the build"
            echo "::error::would NOT fail - it would sign with a throwaway key, and"
            echo "::error::adb install -r over the copy on the phone would then fail with"
            echo "::error::INSTALL_FAILED_UPDATE_INCOMPATIBLE, forcing an uninstall that"
            echo "::error::wipes the pairing secret."
            exit 1
          fi
          mkdir -p keystore
          printf '%s' "$KEY" | base64 -d > keystore/debug.keystore
          echo "Signing key restored."


      - uses: actions/setup-java@cf277c60eb25467037889841efdb72551f06f6c3 # v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: ${{ inputs.java_version || '17' }}
          cache: gradle

      - uses: android-actions/setup-android@9fc6c4e9069bf8d3d10b2204b1fb8f6ef7065407 # v3
        with:
          # Not the default `tools platform-tools`. Google removed the obsolete
          # `tools` package from the SDK repository, so the action's own default
          # makes it run `sdkmanager tools`, get "Failed to find package 'tools'",
          # and fail the job before a line of this project is compiled. Nothing
          # here ever used it; platform-tools is the half that is real.
          packages: platform-tools

      # Unit tests gate the APK. The assertion on the results directory is the
      # point: `testDebugUnitTest` reports NO-SOURCE and exits 0 when it is
      # pointed at a module with no test sources, so a green check here used to
      # be compatible with nothing having run at all.
      - name: Run unit tests
        working-directory: ${{ env.PROJECT_DIR }}
        run: |
          set -uo pipefail
          set -o pipefail
          chmod +x ./gradlew
          if ! ./gradlew testDebugUnitTest --stacktrace 2>&1 | tee test.log; then
            echo "::group::Kotlin errors"
            grep -E "^e: |Unresolved reference|Argument type mismatch|None of the following candidates|Compilation error" test.log | head -80 \
              || echo "(no compiler diagnostics found; see the full log above)"
            echo "::endgroup::"
            exit 1
          fi
          results=app/build/test-results/testDebugUnitTest
          if ! ls "$results"/TEST-*.xml >/dev/null 2>&1; then
            echo "::error::No test results in $results — the test task matched no sources."
            exit 1
          fi
          echo "Test classes executed:"
          ls -1 "$results"/TEST-*.xml
          # Belt and braces: a results file can exist with zero tests in it.
          total=$(grep -ho 'tests="[0-9]*"' "$results"/TEST-*.xml \
                  | grep -o '[0-9]*' | paste -sd+ - | bc)
          echo "Total tests: ${total:-0}"
          if [ "${total:-0}" -lt 1 ]; then
            echo "::error::Test results contain zero tests."
            exit 1
          fi

      - name: Upload test reports
        if: failure()
        uses: actions/upload-artifact@ea165f8d65b6e75b540449e92b4886f43607fa02 # v4
        with:
          name: jarvis-android-test-reports
          path: ${{ env.PROJECT_DIR }}/app/build/reports/tests/**
          if-no-files-found: ignore
          retention-days: 7

      # Debug only. The app is sideloaded over adb and never listed, so there is
      # no distribution channel a release build would serve — and the keystore
      # plumbing that used to live here fed four SIGNING_* variables into a build
      # that contained no signingConfig to read them, producing an unsigned APK
      # that cannot be installed while CI and the README both called it signed.
      - name: Build debug APK
        working-directory: ${{ env.PROJECT_DIR }}
        run: |
          set -uo pipefail
          set -o pipefail
          chmod +x ./gradlew
          if ! ./gradlew assembleDebug --stacktrace 2>&1 | tee build.log; then
            echo "::group::Kotlin errors"
            grep -E "^e: |^w: .*error|Unresolved reference|Compilation error" build.log | head -80 \
              || echo "(no compiler diagnostics found; see the full log above)"
            echo "::endgroup::"
            exit 1
          fi

      - name: Collect APK
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          mkdir -p artifacts
          found=0
          while IFS= read -r apk; do
            cp "$apk" artifacts/
            echo "Found $apk"
            found=1
          done < <(find "$PROJECT_DIR" -name '*.apk' -print)
          if [ "$found" -eq 0 ]; then
            echo "::error::The build reported success but produced no .apk file."
            exit 1
          fi
          ls -lh artifacts

      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@ea165f8d65b6e75b540449e92b4886f43607fa02 # v4
        with:
          name: jarvis-android-debug-apk
          path: artifacts/*.apk
          if-no-files-found: error
          retention-days: 30

      # This workflow no longer publishes a release, and the reason is not
      # tidiness. `android-latest` sat next to `client-latest` with a nearly
      # identical name, and only one of them can talk to the backend:
      # jarvis-android speaks a WebSocket protocol invented before
      # JARVIS-API.md existed, and none of its endpoints are implemented
      # server-side. Two similar links where one silently cannot work is a trap
      # rather than a choice, and installing the wrong one reads as "my phone is
      # broken" rather than "wrong app".
      #
      # The module itself stays, and so does the build above. Its duplex audio,
      # lock-screen approvals and Glance widgets are worth porting into the real
      # client, and a module that still compiles is a much easier thing to port
      # from than one that rotted quietly. The run artifact is still produced
      # for anyone who actually wants this APK.
````

## `.github/workflows/ci.yml`

````yaml
# The 21 unit tests in this repo had nothing to run them until now, and the
# checks below are exactly the ones that were being typed by hand — including
# the two that would have caught real bugs: `cargo test` covers the ported
# colour engine, the SSE frame parser and the process-tree killer, and the
# markdown suite covers a parser that ran on every streamed token.
name: CI

on:
  push:
    branches: ["**"]
  pull_request:

env:
  CARGO_TERM_COLOR: always

jobs:
  rust:
    # The product is a Windows app. Checking it anywhere else would type-check
    # the wrong half of every `#[cfg(windows)]`.
    runs-on: windows-latest
    defaults:
      run:
        working-directory: jarvis-desktop/src-tauri
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: dtolnay/rust-toolchain@stable
        with:
          components: rustfmt, clippy
      - uses: Swatinem/rust-cache@v2
        with:
          workspaces: jarvis-desktop/src-tauri
      - run: cargo fmt --check
      - run: cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings
      - run: cargo test

  frontend:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    defaults:
      run:
        working-directory: jarvis-desktop
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version: "22"
      # No bundler and no test framework: the pages are plain ES modules, so a
      # parse check is what there is. It would have caught nothing in this
      # repo's history — the frontend bugs were behavioural — but it is the
      # floor, and the markdown suite below is the part that matters.
      - run: for f in src/*.js; do node --check "$f"; done
        shell: bash
      - run: node scripts/markdown-test.mjs
      # Two of the ten frontend suites need no browser at all — they parse and
      # compute rather than render — so they belong here rather than only on a
      # machine where someone has installed Playwright. The other eight open
      # real pages and stay out of CI for the reason in tests/README.md.
      - run: python3 scripts/check-tokens.py
      - run: node tests/voicecheck.mjs

  audit:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    defaults:
      run:
        working-directory: jarvis-desktop/src-tauri
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: dtolnay/rust-toolchain@stable
      - uses: taiki-e/install-action@cargo-deny
      - run: cargo deny check licenses advisories
````

## `.github/workflows/jarvis-client.yml`

````yaml
name: Jarvis client

on:
  workflow_dispatch:
  push:
    paths:
      - "jarvis-client/**"
      # The shared debug key this module is now signed with.
      - "keystore/**"
      - ".github/workflows/jarvis-client.yml"

permissions:
  contents: write

concurrency:
  group: jarvis-client-${{ github.ref }}
  cancel-in-progress: true

jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    defaults:
      run:
        working-directory: jarvis-client
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@11d5960a326750d5838078e36cf38b85af677262 # v4
      - name: Put the signing key back
        # From the DEBUG_KEYSTORE_B64 repository secret, not from the repo. The
        # key used to be committed; it was public, so it was removed and
        # replaced. build.gradle.kts still reads keystore/debug.keystore from
        # the repository root, so that is where this writes it.
        #
        # github.workspace is explicit because jarvis-client.yml sets a
        # job-level working-directory of jarvis-client, and this file does not
        # belong there.
        working-directory: ${{ github.workspace }}
        env:
          KEY: ${{ secrets.DEBUG_KEYSTORE_B64 }}
        run: |
          if [ -z "$KEY" ]; then
            echo "::error::The DEBUG_KEYSTORE_B64 secret is missing. Stopping on purpose:"
            echo "::error::build.gradle.kts guards the keystore on exists(), so the build"
            echo "::error::would NOT fail - it would sign with a throwaway key, and"
            echo "::error::adb install -r over the copy on the phone would then fail with"
            echo "::error::INSTALL_FAILED_UPDATE_INCOMPATIBLE, forcing an uninstall that"
            echo "::error::wipes the pairing secret."
            exit 1
          fi
          mkdir -p keystore
          printf '%s' "$KEY" | base64 -d > keystore/debug.keystore
          echo "Signing key restored."


      - uses: actions/setup-java@cf277c60eb25467037889841efdb72551f06f6c3 # v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: "17"
          cache: gradle

      - uses: android-actions/setup-android@9fc6c4e9069bf8d3d10b2204b1fb8f6ef7065407 # v3
        with:
          # Not the default `tools platform-tools`. Google removed the obsolete
          # `tools` package from the SDK repository, so the action's own default
          # makes it run `sdkmanager tools`, get "Failed to find package 'tools'",
          # and fail the job before a line of this project is compiled. Nothing
          # here ever used it; platform-tools is the half that is real.
          packages: platform-tools

      # The client had no test step at all. It now has tests, and the same
      # results-directory assertion as the other workflow, so "no test sources"
      # cannot pass for green.
      - name: Run unit tests
        run: |
          set -uo pipefail
          set -o pipefail
          chmod +x ./gradlew
          if ! ./gradlew testDebugUnitTest --stacktrace 2>&1 | tee test.log; then
            echo "::group::Kotlin errors"
            grep -E "^e: |Unresolved reference|Argument type mismatch|None of the following candidates|Compilation error" test.log | head -80 \
              || echo "(none - so this is a TEST failure, not a compile failure)"
            echo "::endgroup::"
            # Which tests failed, and why. Gradle prints the assertion above a
            # hundred lines of its own stack trace, so a failing test used to
            # read as "no compiler diagnostics found" and nothing else - the
            # names were in the log and effectively unfindable.
            echo "::group::Failing tests"
            grep -E "FAILED$|^[A-Za-z].*> .* FAILED" test.log | head -40 || true
            results=app/build/test-results/testDebugUnitTest
            for f in "$results"/TEST-*.xml; do
              [ -e "$f" ] || continue
              python3 - "$f" <<'PYEOF' || true
          import sys, xml.etree.ElementTree as ET
          try:
              root = ET.parse(sys.argv[1]).getroot()
          except Exception:
              sys.exit(0)
          for case in root.iter("testcase"):
              for bad in list(case.findall("failure")) + list(case.findall("error")):
                  print(f'  {case.get("classname")}.{case.get("name")}')
                  # Up to 25 lines, not one. An assertion that lists WHICH
                  # cases disagreed puts the count on line one and the cases
                  # underneath, so printing `msg[0]` reported "8 of 113
                  # vectors" and threw away all eight. The smoke job has
                  # printed 25 since it was written; this half never caught up.
                  # `message` FIRST, not `text`. The attribute is the
                  # assertion alone; the element body is the assertion plus
                  # the whole stack trace. Preferring `text` meant an
                  # assertion that lists eight cases printed one line of
                  # message, eight lines of detail, and then sixteen frames of
                  # JUnit reflection - and the log tail cut the top off, so
                  # the eight were lost a second time by the fix meant to
                  # surface them. Frames are the one thing never in doubt.
                  body = (bad.get("message") or bad.text or "").strip()
                  if not body:
                      body = bad.get("type", "failed")
                  for line in body.splitlines()[:25]:
                      print(f'      {line[:300]}')
          PYEOF
            done
            echo "::endgroup::"
            exit 1
          fi
          results=app/build/test-results/testDebugUnitTest
          if ! ls "$results"/TEST-*.xml >/dev/null 2>&1; then
            echo "::error::No test results in $results — the test task matched no sources."
            exit 1
          fi
          total=$(grep -ho 'tests="[0-9]*"' "$results"/TEST-*.xml \
                  | grep -o '[0-9]*' | paste -sd+ - | bc)
          echo "Total tests: ${total:-0}"
          if [ "${total:-0}" -lt 1 ]; then
            echo "::error::Test results contain zero tests."
            exit 1
          fi

      - name: Upload test reports
        if: failure()
        uses: actions/upload-artifact@ea165f8d65b6e75b540449e92b4886f43607fa02 # v4
        with:
          name: jarvis-client-test-reports
          path: jarvis-client/app/build/reports/tests/**
          if-no-files-found: ignore
          retention-days: 7

      - name: Assemble debug APK
        run: |
          set -uo pipefail
          set -o pipefail
          chmod +x ./gradlew
          if ! ./gradlew assembleDebug --stacktrace 2>&1 | tee build.log; then
            echo "::group::Kotlin errors"
            grep -E "^e: |^w: .*error|Unresolved reference|Compilation error" build.log | head -80 \
              || echo "(no compiler diagnostics found; see the full log above)"
            echo "::endgroup::"
            exit 1
          fi

      - name: Collect APK
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          mkdir -p artifacts
          find . -name '*.apk' -not -path './artifacts/*' -exec cp {} artifacts/ \;
          ls -lh artifacts

      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@ea165f8d65b6e75b540449e92b4886f43607fa02 # v4
        with:
          name: jarvis-client-debug-apk
          path: jarvis-client/artifacts/*.apk
          if-no-files-found: error
          retention-days: 30

      # After the debug artifact is collected, so `find -name '*.apk'` above
      # keeps picking up exactly one file.
      #
      # The release variant is now the one that gets published, but not from
      # here. R8 breaks at RUNTIME, not at build time - a stripped serializer
      # is a crash on first decode and a green `assembleRelease` - so the
      # publish sits behind the smoke job, which installs and starts the
      # shrunk APK on a real device first. This step earns its place anyway:
      # it fails a broken shrink in about three minutes without waiting for an
      # emulator to boot, and it prints the two sizes side by side.
      - name: Assemble release APK
        run: |
          set -uo pipefail
          set -o pipefail
          chmod +x ./gradlew
          if ! ./gradlew assembleRelease --stacktrace 2>&1 | tee release.log; then
            echo "::group::Why R8 refused"
            # R8's own complaints, which are not Kotlin diagnostics and do not
            # look like them. A missing keep rule shows up here as a warning
            # about a class it could not find a reference for.
            grep -E "^e: |Missing class|R8: |Warning: |Error: |ProGuard" release.log | head -60               || echo "(nothing that looks like an R8 complaint; see the full log above)"
            echo "::endgroup::"
            exit 1
          fi
          echo "::group::Size"
          debug=$(find app/build/outputs/apk/debug -name '*.apk' | head -1)
          rel=$(find app/build/outputs/apk/release -name '*.apk' | head -1)
          for f in "$debug" "$rel"; do
            [ -n "$f" ] && [ -e "$f" ] && printf '%10s  %s\n' "$(du -h "$f" | cut -f1)" "$f"
          done
          echo "::endgroup::"

  # The gap that let a crash in onCreate ship with a green tick on every
  # check: the job above compiles the APK and runs unit tests, and never once
  # launches it. This one does, on a real Android image — and because an APK
  # that cannot start is not a release, publishing moved here behind it.
  #
  # No third-party emulator action, deliberately. Every action in this file is
  # pinned to a SHA so it cannot be swapped underneath us, and the one I
  # reached for could not have its SHA verified from where this was written —
  # so it is thirty lines of shell against the SDK that the already-pinned
  # setup-android installs. Publishing from this job rather than a third
  # avoids an artifact download, which would have meant another unverifiable
  # pin for no gain.
  smoke:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    needs: build
    timeout-minutes: 35
    env:
      # Pinned explicitly because the default is not one path but three, tried
      # in order — $ANDROID_AVD_HOME, $ANDROID_SDK_HOME/avd, $HOME/.android/avd
      # — and avdmanager and the emulator resolved it differently between
      # steps, so a created AVD was simply not there a minute later.
      ANDROID_AVD_HOME: ${{ github.workspace }}/.avd
    defaults:
      run:
        working-directory: jarvis-client
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@11d5960a326750d5838078e36cf38b85af677262 # v4

      - uses: actions/setup-java@cf277c60eb25467037889841efdb72551f06f6c3 # v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: "17"
          cache: gradle

      - uses: android-actions/setup-android@9fc6c4e9069bf8d3d10b2204b1fb8f6ef7065407 # v3
        with:
          # Not the default `tools platform-tools`. Google removed the obsolete
          # `tools` package from the SDK repository, so the action's own default
          # makes it run `sdkmanager tools`, get "Failed to find package 'tools'",
          # and fail the job before a line of this project is compiled. Nothing
          # here ever used it; platform-tools is the half that is real.
          packages: platform-tools

      # Without this the emulator drops to pure software emulation and takes
      # long enough to boot that the wait below times out.
      - name: Let the runner use KVM
        working-directory: .
        run: |
          echo 'KERNEL=="kvm", GROUP="kvm", MODE="0666", OPTIONS+="static_node=kvm"' \
            | sudo tee /etc/udev/rules.d/99-kvm4all.rules
          sudo udevadm control --reload-rules
          sudo udevadm trigger --name-match=kvm

      - name: Install the system image
        working-directory: .
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          yes | sdkmanager --licenses >/dev/null 2>&1 || true
          sdkmanager --install "system-images;android-34;default;x86_64" \
                               "platforms;android-34" "platform-tools" "emulator"
          # "minutes" versus "tens of minutes" is exactly the difference
          # between having this and not, and it is worth seeing before a
          # timeout rather than after one.
          ls -l /dev/kvm 2>&1 || echo "::warning::/dev/kvm is absent - the emulator will be very slow"

      # Compiled before the device is needed, so a Kotlin error still fails in
      # about a minute rather than after a boot.
      #
      # `assembleRelease` is here too, because the RELEASE apk is the one this
      # job now publishes, and it has to exist on disk before the emulator is
      # up. R8 takes about three and a half minutes; failing that here costs a
      # boot cycle less than failing it later.
      - name: Compile the app and the test
        run: |
          set -uo pipefail
          chmod +x ./gradlew
          if ! ./gradlew assembleDebug assembleDebugAndroidTest assembleRelease --stacktrace 2>&1 | tee build.log; then
            echo "::group::Kotlin errors, and R8's"
            # R8's complaints are not Kotlin diagnostics and look nothing like
            # them: a missing keep rule appears as a warning about a class it
            # could not resolve a reference for.
            grep -E "^e: |Unresolved reference|Argument type mismatch|Compilation error|Missing class|R8: " build.log | head -80 \
              || echo "(no compiler diagnostics found; see the full log above)"
            echo "::endgroup::"
            exit 1
          fi

      # Create, start, wait and test in ONE step. Everything this job has got
      # wrong so far has been state that did not survive a step boundary: the
      # emulator died with the process group that launched it, and then the AVD
      # itself was not where the next step looked for it. Nothing crosses a
      # boundary now except the installed SDK, which is on disk.
      - name: Start the emulator and launch the app
        run: |
          # `-e` is NOT set here, and is on anyway: GitHub runs every `run:`
          # block as `bash -e {0}`. That matters more than it looks. With `-e`
          # and `pipefail` both on, ANY command substitution whose pipeline
          # ends non-zero kills the step on the spot - including a `x=$(...)`
          # assignment written as a question rather than as an action. That is
          # what silently killed this step for three merges; see the pid check
          # at the bottom.
          set -uo pipefail
          mkdir -p "$ANDROID_AVD_HOME"
          if ! echo no | avdmanager create avd -n smoke \
            -k "system-images;android-34;default;x86_64" --force; then
            echo "::error::The AVD could not be created, so there is no device to test on."
            exit 1
          fi

          # Guest Vulkan off, before the emulator is launched.
          #
          # The emulator draws for the guest through a host component called
          # gfxstream, and "Failed to find ColorBuffer: <n>" - which the
          # emulator log tail has carried on every failed run of this job - is
          # gfxstream being handed a handle for a graphics buffer it no longer
          # has. Leaving Vulkan available to the guest gives it a second way to
          # allocate those buffers, and turning it off is the standard
          # workaround for this error on a runner with no GPU. Nothing in this
          # app uses Vulkan - Compose and the GLES faces both go through
          # OpenGL - so there is nothing here for it to take away.
          #
          # This file is an override layer on top of the SDK's own
          # emulator/lib/advancedFeatures.ini, so naming two features leaves
          # every other default alone. It is read from $HOME/.android;
          # ANDROID_AVD_HOME above moves the AVDs, not this.
          #
          # BEST-EFFORT. I cannot run an emulator here to confirm it, and the
          # evidence below says the graphics stack is probably not what is
          # failing anyway. It is cheap, it is documented, and it narrows what
          # the next run has to explain.
          mkdir -p "$HOME/.android"
          printf 'Vulkan = off\nGLDirectMem = on\n' >> "$HOME/.android/advancedFeatures.ini"
          echo "Emulator feature overrides:"; cat "$HOME/.android/advancedFeatures.ini"

          # Assert it exists HERE, so a creation that silently went elsewhere
          # fails on the line that created it rather than five minutes later
          # inside the emulator's own error message.
          if ! "$ANDROID_HOME/emulator/emulator" -list-avds | grep -qx smoke; then
            echo "::error::The AVD was created but the emulator cannot see it."
            echo "ANDROID_AVD_HOME=$ANDROID_AVD_HOME"; ls -la "$ANDROID_AVD_HOME" || true
            "$ANDROID_HOME/emulator/emulator" -list-avds || true
            exit 1
          fi

          # `-gpu swiftshader_indirect` is KEPT, deliberately, and this is the
          # evidence for not churning it: `FaceRenderTest` launches the app
          # once per face in `Faces.all` on this very emulator - including the
          # `GLSurfaceView` faces, which ask for real GLES 3.0 and an
          # RGBA8888 + 16-bit-depth config, and the AGSL face, whose shader is
          # compiled at runtime by the device's own Skia - and asserts both
          # that the activity reaches RESUMED and that `GL.lastBuildFailure`
          # is null. Those tests pass on every run of this job. Software GL is
          # demonstrably able to serve this app's shaders on this image, so
          # swapping the renderer would be a guess that risks breaking the one
          # part of this job that works.
          #
          # -memory 4096, up from 2048. A GitHub ubuntu-latest runner has 16GB,
          # and a 2GB Android device that has just finished running a whole
          # instrumentation suite can have Android's low-memory killer take
          # the next app that starts - which looks EXACTLY like the failure
          # here (process gone, nothing in the crash buffer, because a
          # low-memory kill is not a crash and writes nothing there). A guess,
          # but a free one: the exit-info dump added below will say
          # outright if that is what happened.
          "$ANDROID_HOME/emulator/emulator" -avd smoke \
            -no-window -no-audio -no-boot-anim -no-snapshot -no-metrics \
            -camera-back none -camera-front none -memory 4096 \
            -gpu swiftshader_indirect -accel auto > "$RUNNER_TEMP/emulator.log" 2>&1 &
          EMU=$!

          finish() {
            # Twelve lines, not a hundred and twenty. This runs on EXIT, so it
            # prints AFTER the failing test names - and GitHub's log API serves
            # a tail, so 120 lines of emulator boot properties pushed the one
            # useful thing out of every window I could fetch. Four log pulls
            # across two runs said only "a test failed" because of this trap.
            #
            # The emulator's own failures are at the end of its log anyway
            # (boot completed, panics, driver errors); the boot-property dump
            # that made up the bulk of it has never once been the answer.
            echo "::group::Emulator log (tail)"
            tail -12 "$RUNNER_TEMP/emulator.log" 2>/dev/null || echo "(none)"
            echo "::endgroup::"
            kill "$EMU" 2>/dev/null || true
          }
          trap finish EXIT

          if ! timeout 300 adb wait-for-device; then
            echo "::error::The emulator never registered with adb."
            exit 1
          fi
          if ! timeout 600 adb shell 'while [ "$(getprop sys.boot_completed)" != "1" ]; do sleep 2; done'; then
            echo "::error::The emulator registered but never finished booting."
            exit 1
          fi
          adb shell input keyevent 82 || true
          adb devices

          if ! ./gradlew connectedDebugAndroidTest --stacktrace 2>&1 | tee smoke.log; then
            # Ordered noisiest-first, so the thing worth reading is LAST.
            # GitHub's log API serves a tail, and the emulator prints ~110
            # lines of boot properties on its way out - enough to push the
            # failing test names out of every window I could fetch. Three
            # separate log pulls said only "a test failed" because of it.
            echo "::group::Crash log, if the app got that far"
            adb logcat -d -b crash | tail -80 || true
            echo "::endgroup::"

            echo "::group::What the device said"
            # The assertion message carries the stack trace the app itself
            # recorded, so this is usually already the answer.
            grep -E "FAILED|something threw|AssertionError|Caused by" smoke.log | head -40 || true

            # And the instrumentation report, because none of the above is in
            # stdout. Gradle prints "There were failing tests. See the report
            # at: file://..." and nothing else - a local path, on a runner that
            # is about to be destroyed. The first time this job went red the
            # whole failure read as one Gradle exception with no test name in
            # it, and the HTML report was only reachable as a build artifact
            # from a blob host. The names are in the XML sitting right here.
            #
            # Python walks the tree itself rather than `for f in $(find ...)`.
            # The instrumentation report is named after the device - the file
            # is `TEST-smoke(AVD) - 14.xml`, spaces and parentheses included -
            # and word-splitting that is what broke the test-count check two
            # commits below this one. Not repeating it here.
            python3 - <<'PYEOF' || true
          import os, xml.etree.ElementTree as ET
          for root_dir, _, names in os.walk("app/build"):
              if "ndroidTest" not in root_dir:
                  continue
              for name in names:
                  if not (name.startswith("TEST-") and name.endswith(".xml")):
                      continue
                  try:
                      tree = ET.parse(os.path.join(root_dir, name)).getroot()
                  except Exception:
                      continue
                  for case in tree.iter("testcase"):
                      for bad in list(case.findall("failure")) + list(case.findall("error")):
                          print(f'  {case.get("classname")}.{case.get("name")}')
                          body = (bad.text or bad.get("message") or "").strip()
                          for line in body.splitlines()[:25]:
                              print(f'      {line}')
          PYEOF

            echo "::endgroup::"
            exit 1
          fi

          # The same assertion the unit-test job has, and for the same reason.
          # `connectedDebugAndroidTest` prints "Running tests on devices" and
          # exits 0 whether it ran three tests or none, so without this a test
          # class that stopped being discovered — a renamed runner, a manifest
          # merge that dropped it, a filter — would read as a passing smoke
          # test. That is precisely the shape of the hole this whole job exists
          # to close, and leaving it open here would have been the same mistake
          # one level up.
          #
          # -print0/xargs -0 rather than an unquoted variable, because the
          # instrumentation report is named after the DEVICE — the file here is
          # `TEST-smoke(AVD) - 14.xml`, spaces and parentheses included. Passing
          # that through word splitting is what broke the first version of this
          # check: the tests had passed, and the assertion written to prove they
          # ran is what failed the job.
          #
          # Counting `<testcase` rather than summing `tests="N"`, because that
          # attribute appears TWICE in one instrumentation report — once on the
          # `<testsuites>` wrapper and once on the `<testsuite>` inside it — so
          # summing every match reported 16 for the 8 tests that ran. A
          # `<testcase` element is one test, once, in either layout.
          #
          # I first blamed a duplicate file and deduplicated by filename. That
          # was wrong, and the line below that prints what it counted is what
          # showed it: one path, still 16. The dedupe is kept because it is
          # harmless and correct for genuinely multiple devices, but it is not
          # what fixed this.
          #
          # The paths are printed for exactly that reason. A number with
          # nothing behind it cannot be checked, and this one was wrong twice
          # before anything said so.
          reports=$(find app/build -path '*ndroidTest*' -name 'TEST-*.xml' -printf '%f\t%p\n' 2>/dev/null \
                    | sort -t"$(printf '\t')" -u -k1,1 | cut -f2- || true)
          if [ -z "$reports" ]; then
            echo "::error::No instrumentation results were written — nothing ran on the device."
            find app/build -name 'TEST-*.xml' 2>/dev/null || true
            exit 1
          fi
          echo "Counting:"; printf '%s\n' "$reports" | sed 's/^/  /'
          # The `|| true` is inside the braces so it covers grep finding
          # nothing — which is the case being guarded against — without letting
          # the step die on grep's exit status under -e and pipefail, and
          # without printing a second number after wc has already printed one.
          total=$( { printf '%s\n' "$reports" | tr '\n' '\0' \
                     | xargs -0 -r grep -oh '<testcase[ >]' || true; } | wc -l )
          echo "Tests run on the device: ${total:-0}"
          if [ "${total:-0}" -lt 1 ]; then
            echo "::error::The instrumentation report contains zero tests."
            exit 1
          fi

          # ---------------------------------------------------------------
          # The release build, started on the device, before it is published.
          #
          # This is the gate that lets the published artifact BE the release
          # APK. R8 removes code it cannot prove is reachable, and everything
          # it gets wrong fails at RUNTIME rather than at build time: a
          # stripped serializer is a crash on first decode and a perfectly
          # green `assembleRelease`. Publishing an APK no machine has ever
          # started is the same hole this whole job exists to close, one build
          # type across.
          #
          # Be exact about what this proves, because it is less than the suite
          # above proves. The instrumentation tests still run against DEBUG:
          # testing a minified variant means threading the app's R8 mapping
          # into the test APK, which is a change worth making deliberately and
          # not folded into this one. So this establishes that the release APK
          # installs, starts, stays up, and logs none of R8's failure
          # signatures. It does not walk every screen. A keep rule missing for
          # something only reachable three taps in would still get through.
          echo "::group::The release build on the device"
          # `|| true` on the assignment, not decoration. If the release
          # directory does not exist, `find` exits non-zero, `pipefail` hands
          # that status to the whole pipeline, and under `-e` the ASSIGNMENT
          # is the failing command - so the step would die here and the
          # friendly message two lines down, written for exactly this case,
          # would never be printed. Same bug as the pid check below, same fix.
          rel=$(find app/build/outputs/apk/release -name 'app-release.apk' 2>/dev/null | head -1) || true
          if [ -z "$rel" ] || [ ! -e "$rel" ]; then
            echo "::error::No release APK was built, so there is nothing to smoke or publish."
            find app/build/outputs/apk -name '*.apk' 2>/dev/null || true
            exit 1
          fi
          echo "Installing $rel"

          # Uninstalled first, so this is a FRESH install with no data behind
          # it - which is the case a person sideloading hits, and the one where
          # a missing keep rule shows up on the very first screen rather than
          # never.
          adb uninstall com.jarvis.client >/dev/null 2>&1 || true
          out=$(adb install -r "$rel" 2>&1) || true
          printf '%s\n' "$out"
          case "$out" in
            *Success*) ;;
            *) echo "::error::The release APK would not install."; exit 1 ;;
          esac

          # `-b all`, not a bare `-c`. The crash-buffer check at the end of
          # this gate is only meaningful if the buffer starts empty, and the
          # instrumentation suite has been running on this same device for the
          # last few minutes.
          adb logcat -b all -c 2>/dev/null || adb logcat -c || true
          out=$(adb shell am start -W -n com.jarvis.client/.MainActivity 2>&1) || true
          printf '%s\n' "$out"
          case "$out" in
            *Error*|*Exception*) echo "::error::The release APK would not start."; exit 1 ;;
          esac

          # ---------------------------------------------------------------
          # Is it still running? This is the check that broke the job, and it
          # broke by dying rather than by reporting.
          #
          # It used to be one line:
          #
          #   pid=$(adb shell pidof com.jarvis.client 2>/dev/null | ... )
          #
          # `pidof` exits 1 when it finds no process, `adb shell` passes the
          # remote exit code back, `pipefail` makes that the status of the
          # whole pipeline, and under `-e` (which GitHub sets on every step)
          # an assignment whose command substitution failed IS a failed
          # command. So the step exited 1 ON THAT LINE - before the `if` below
          # it, which is where the explanation lived. The EXIT trap still ran,
          # so every failed run showed twelve lines of emulator log and
          # "Process completed with exit code 1", and the app's actual fate
          # was never printed because nothing ever asked about it. Three
          # merges went past this reading the emulator log as the culprit.
          #
          # Two guards, and they do different jobs. `|| true` INSIDE the guest
          # shell makes "no such process" a normal answer rather than a
          # failure, which leaves adb's own non-zero exit meaning the one
          # thing it should: adb could not reach the device. And the sampling
          # below tests the assignment with `if ! pid=$(...)`, because a
          # command in an `if` condition is exempt from `-e` - so a dead
          # device is a branch to report on instead of a step that stops
          # mid-sentence. Nothing here is weaker than it was: the app still
          # has to be running at the end of the same fifteen seconds. It just
          # says which thing went wrong.
          sample_pid() {
            adb shell 'pidof com.jarvis.client || true' 2>/dev/null \
              | tr -d '\r' | awk '{print $1}'
          }

          # Everything worth knowing about a process that is no longer there.
          # `exit-info` first because it is three lines and usually decisive:
          # Android records WHY it reaped each process, so CRASH, ANR,
          # LOW_MEMORY and "the shell killed it" stop being guesses.
          explain_death() {
            echo "--- what Android says ended the process ---"
            adb shell dumpsys activity exit-info com.jarvis.client 2>/dev/null | head -40 || true
            echo "--- crash buffer ---"
            adb logcat -d -b crash 2>/dev/null | tail -60 || true
            echo "--- anything about this app, or about memory pressure ---"
            { adb logcat -d 2>/dev/null \
              | grep -iE "jarvis|AndroidRuntime|lowmemorykiller|lmkd|am_kill|ANR in" \
              | tail -60; } || true
            echo "--- the emulator's own complaints ---"
            { grep -iE "panic|fatal|abort|ERROR" "$RUNNER_TEMP/emulator.log" | tail -20; } || true
          }

          # Sampled across the same fifteen seconds instead of one `sleep 15`
          # and one look. The window is unchanged and so is the assertion; the
          # difference is that "it never had a process", "it died at +6s" and
          # "it died and something restarted it" are three different bugs, and
          # a single sample at the end could not tell them apart - the last of
          # them it could not see at all, because a restarted app has a pid.
          first_pid=""
          pid=""
          gone_at=""
          adb_down=""
          elapsed=0
          for _ in 1 2 3 4 5; do
            sleep 3
            elapsed=$((elapsed + 3))
            if ! pid=$(sample_pid); then
              adb_down=$elapsed
              pid=""
              break
            fi
            if [ -n "$pid" ] && [ -z "$first_pid" ]; then
              first_pid=$pid
            fi
            if [ -z "$pid" ] && [ -z "$gone_at" ]; then
              gone_at=$elapsed
            fi
          done

          if [ -n "$adb_down" ]; then
            echo "::error::The device stopped answering adb ${adb_down}s after the release APK was started."
            echo "::error::That is the EMULATOR going away, not the app crashing - the two"
            echo "::error::look identical from here, which is why this case is reported separately."
            adb devices || true
            tail -40 "$RUNNER_TEMP/emulator.log" 2>/dev/null || true
            exit 1
          fi
          if [ -z "$first_pid" ]; then
            echo "::error::The release APK was started but never had a running process."
            echo "::error::'am start' reported success, so this is the process dying within"
            echo "::error::three seconds of launch - the shape a stripped class takes."
            explain_death
            exit 1
          fi
          if [ -z "$pid" ]; then
            echo "::error::The release APK started and then died (gone by ${gone_at}s)."
            explain_death
            exit 1
          fi
          if [ "$pid" != "$first_pid" ]; then
            echo "::error::The release APK crashed and was restarted inside the 15s window"
            echo "::error::(started as pid $first_pid, now pid $pid)."
            explain_death
            exit 1
          fi
          echo "Still alive after 15s, pid $pid"

          # R8 damage the app CAUGHT still counts. This app has a crash screen,
          # so a stripped class can surface as a handled exception and a live
          # process - which passes a "did it survive" check while being exactly
          # the bug this gate is for. These are the shapes code removal takes at
          # runtime; none of them has an innocent reading inside our own pid.
          adb logcat -d --pid="$pid" > "$RUNNER_TEMP/app.log" 2>/dev/null || true
          bad=$( { grep -nE "FATAL EXCEPTION|ClassNotFoundException|NoSuchMethodError|NoSuchFieldError|ExceptionInInitializerError|Serializer for class|SerializationException" \
                   "$RUNNER_TEMP/app.log" || true; } | head -20 )
          if [ -n "$bad" ]; then
            echo "::error::The release build logged what code removal looks like at runtime."
            printf '%s\n' "$bad"
            exit 1
          fi

          # And the crash buffer, for a process that died and restarted inside
          # the window above - which the pid check alone would read as healthy.
          crashed=$( { adb logcat -d -b crash 2>/dev/null | grep -c "com.jarvis.client" || true; } )
          if [ "${crashed:-0}" -gt 0 ]; then
            echo "::error::The release build wrote to the crash buffer."
            adb logcat -d -b crash | tail -60 || true
            exit 1
          fi
          echo "The release APK installs, starts and stays up."
          echo "::endgroup::"

      - name: Upload the report
        if: failure()
        uses: actions/upload-artifact@ea165f8d65b6e75b540449e92b4886f43607fa02 # v4
        with:
          name: jarvis-client-smoke-report
          path: jarvis-client/app/build/reports/androidTests/**
          if-no-files-found: ignore
          retention-days: 7

      # The WHOLE emulator log, not the twelve lines the trap prints. Those
      # twelve are the right thing in the log - they are what is read first
      # and they must not be buried - but they are a tail, and three failed
      # runs were diagnosed from them alone, wrongly. This costs nothing on a
      # green run and means the next red one can be read properly.
      - name: Upload the emulator log
        if: failure()
        uses: actions/upload-artifact@ea165f8d65b6e75b540449e92b4886f43607fa02 # v4
        with:
          name: jarvis-client-emulator-log
          path: ${{ runner.temp }}/emulator.log
          if-no-files-found: ignore
          retention-days: 7

      # The RELEASE apk, which the step above has just started on a real
      # device. `app-release.apk` and not `*.apk`: the debug build and the
      # androidTest build are both sitting in the same tree, and picking the
      # wrong one here would publish a debuggable APK under release notes
      # saying otherwise - which is worse than publishing the debug one openly.
      - name: Collect APK
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          mkdir -p artifacts
          find . -name 'app-release.apk' -not -path './artifacts/*' -exec cp {} artifacts/ \;
          if [ ! -e artifacts/app-release.apk ]; then
            echo "::error::No release APK to publish."
            exit 1
          fi
          ls -lh artifacts

      # This is the app that actually talks to Jarvis, so it gets the same
      # phone-reachable delivery as the other one: a plain .apk at a stable URL
      # rather than a zip buried in a run the GitHub mobile app cannot download.
      - name: Publish the APK to a rolling release
        id: release
        continue-on-error: true
        working-directory: .
        env:
          GH_TOKEN: ${{ github.token }}
          TAG: client-latest
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          short="${GITHUB_SHA::7}"
          mv jarvis-client/artifacts/app-release.apk "jarvis-client/artifacts/jarvis-client-$short.apk"
          size=$(du -h "jarvis-client/artifacts/jarvis-client-$short.apk" | cut -f1)

          notes=$(printf '%s\n' \
            "Release build of \`jarvis-client\` from commit \`$short\` on \`$GITHUB_REF_NAME\`. **$size.**" \
            "" \
            "The only app. It speaks the real Jarvis API - SSE on \`/api/events\` plus REST, authenticated with \`X-Jarvis-Token\`." \
            "" \
            '```' \
            "adb install -r jarvis-client-$short.apk" \
            '```' \
            "" \
            "Or open this page on the phone and tap the \`.apk\`." \
            "" \
            "### What changed about these builds" \
            "" \
            "This is the shrunk, optimised build, not the debug one. It is roughly a tenth the size, and Android is allowed to compile it ahead of time instead of interpreting every class - so the reactor face and the chat stream have a real frame budget for the first time. It also closes \`adb shell run-as\`, which on a debug build let anything with adb read the app's data directory, pairing token included." \
            "" \
            "### What was checked before this was published" \
            "" \
            "The unit tests and the instrumentation suite both passed, on an Android 34 emulator. Then THIS apk - the shrunk one, not a rebuild of it - was installed fresh on that emulator, started, and watched for fifteen seconds. It has to still be running, with nothing in its log that looks like the shrinker removed code it needed, or nothing is published." \
            "" \
            "Honest limit: that proves it starts and the first screen works. It does not walk every screen, so a shrinker fault on something only reachable several taps in would still get through. Report anything that crashes only on this build and not the previous debug one." \
            "" \
            "Signed with the committed debug key, the same one every previous build used, so it installs over an existing copy in place with no uninstall and no loss of pairing. This tag is rolling.")

          gh release delete "$TAG" --cleanup-tag --yes 2>/dev/null || true
          gh release create "$TAG" jarvis-client/artifacts/*.apk \
            --prerelease \
            --target "$GITHUB_SHA" \
            --title "Jarvis client - latest release build" \
            --notes "$notes"

          echo "Published: $(gh release view "$TAG" --json url --jq .url)"

      - name: Explain a failed release
        if: steps.release.outcome == 'failure'
        working-directory: .
        run: |
          echo "::warning::Could not publish the release. If this was a 403, set" \
               "Settings -> Actions -> General -> Workflow permissions to" \
               "'Read and write permissions'. The APK is still available as the" \
               "jarvis-client-debug-apk run artifact."
````

## `.github/workflows/verify-toolchain.yml`

````yaml
name: Verify toolchain claims

# Settles questions that cannot be answered from the dev container: its egress
# policy blocks dl.google.com and services.gradle.org, and there is no Android
# SDK there. Runners have both, so the answers are measured rather than assumed.
#
# Everything is teed to stdout as well as the step summary — the run log does not
# carry the summary, so results written only there are unreadable from the CLI.

on:
  workflow_dispatch:
  push:
    paths: [".github/workflows/verify-toolchain.yml"]

permissions:
  contents: read

jobs:
  probe:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@11d5960a326750d5838078e36cf38b85af677262 # v4

      # Needed to pin every `uses:` to an immutable commit rather than a tag that
      # its owner can move under us — with a keystore or any other secret in job
      # scope, a retagged third-party action reads it straight out of environ.
      - name: Resolve action tags to commit SHAs
        env:
          GH_TOKEN: ${{ github.token }}
        run: |
          {
            echo "## Action tag → commit SHA"
            echo
            echo '| action | tag | sha |'
            echo '| --- | --- | --- |'
            for spec in \
              "actions/checkout:v4" \
              "actions/setup-java:v4" \
              "actions/upload-artifact:v4" \
              "android-actions/setup-android:v3"; do
              repo="${spec%:*}"; tag="${spec##*:}"
              sha=$(curl -sS -H "Authorization: Bearer $GH_TOKEN" \
                    "https://api.github.com/repos/$repo/git/ref/tags/$tag" \
                    | python3 -c 'import sys,json; d=json.load(sys.stdin); o=d.get("object",{}); print(o.get("sha","?"))')
              # An annotated tag resolves to a tag object; dereference it.
              typ=$(curl -sS -H "Authorization: Bearer $GH_TOKEN" \
                    "https://api.github.com/repos/$repo/git/ref/tags/$tag" \
                    | python3 -c 'import sys,json; print(json.load(sys.stdin).get("object",{}).get("type","?"))')
              if [ "$typ" = "tag" ]; then
                sha=$(curl -sS -H "Authorization: Bearer $GH_TOKEN" \
                      "https://api.github.com/repos/$repo/git/tags/$sha" \
                      | python3 -c 'import sys,json; print(json.load(sys.stdin).get("object",{}).get("sha","?"))')
              fi
              echo "| $repo | $tag | $sha |"
            done
          } | tee -a "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"

      # Committing a wrong checksum breaks every build, so these are read from
      # Gradle rather than guessed.
      - name: Gradle distribution checksums
        run: |
          {
            echo
            echo "## Gradle distribution SHA-256"
            echo
            for v in 8.11.1 9.6.0; do
              sum=$(curl -sSL "https://services.gradle.org/distributions/gradle-$v-bin.zip.sha256" || echo unavailable)
              echo "- gradle-$v-bin.zip: \`$sum\`"
            done
          } | tee -a "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"

      # androidx.activity is not managed by the Compose BOM, so pairing a 2026
      # Compose runtime with activity-compose 1.9.3 (Nov 2024) is a real pin, not
      # a cosmetic one — under targetSdk 36, where edge-to-edge enforcement
      # changed and MainActivity calls enableEdgeToEdge().
      - name: Published androidx.activity versions
        shell: bash {0}
        run: |
          {
            echo
            echo "## androidx.activity:activity-compose — published versions"
            echo
            curl -sS "https://dl.google.com/dl/android/maven2/androidx/activity/activity-compose/maven-metadata.xml" \
              | grep -o '<version>[^<]*</version>' \
              | sed 's|</\?version>||g' \
              | tail -30 \
              | tr '\n' ' '
            echo
            echo
            echo "Latest: $(curl -sS 'https://dl.google.com/dl/android/maven2/androidx/activity/activity-compose/maven-metadata.xml' | grep -o '<latest>[^<]*</latest>' | sed 's|</\?latest>||g')"
          } | tee -a "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"

      # Last, and compact: the resolution dump below runs to well over a thousand
      # lines, which buries everything above it in the run log.
      - name: Answers
        run: |
          echo "=============== PROBE ANSWERS ==============="
          cat "$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY"
          echo "============= END PROBE ANSWERS ============="

  resolved:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@11d5960a326750d5838078e36cf38b85af677262 # v4

      - uses: actions/setup-java@cf277c60eb25467037889841efdb72551f06f6c3 # v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: "17"
          cache: gradle

      - uses: android-actions/setup-android@9fc6c4e9069bf8d3d10b2204b1fb8f6ef7065407 # v3
        with:
          # Not the default `tools platform-tools`. Google removed the obsolete
          # `tools` package from the SDK repository, so the action's own default
          # makes it run `sdkmanager tools`, get "Failed to find package 'tools'",
          # and fail the job before a line of this project is compiled. Nothing
          # here ever used it; platform-tools is the half that is real.
          packages: platform-tools

      # androidx.activity is not managed by the Compose BOM, so this is where a
      # stale pin shows up as a real number rather than a guess.
      - name: What jarvis-client actually resolves
        working-directory: jarvis-client
        run: |
          set -euo pipefail
          chmod +x ./gradlew
          ./gradlew :app:dependencies --configuration debugRuntimeClasspath > deps.txt
          echo "=============== TOP-LEVEL RESOLUTION ==============="
          grep -E '^\+--- |^\\--- ' deps.txt | sort -u
          echo "=============== ANDROIDX ACTIVITY ==============="
          grep -E 'androidx\.activity' deps.txt | sort -u | head -20
````

## `jarvis-client/app/build.gradle.kts`

````kotlin
plugins {
    id("com.android.application")
    id("org.jetbrains.kotlin.plugin.compose")
    id("org.jetbrains.kotlin.plugin.serialization")
}

android {
    namespace = "com.jarvis.client"
    compileSdk = 36

    defaultConfig {
        applicationId = "com.jarvis.client"
        // 33, not 30: RuntimeShader/AGSL for the Nucleus face needs 33, and
        // taking 30 would mean a GLES fallback branch carried forever.
        minSdk = 33
        targetSdk = 36
        versionCode = 1
        // Not "step0" any more. The number is cosmetic — versionCode is
        // pinned at 1 so any build installs over any other — but a version
        // string naming a step this app passed long ago is one more thing
        // quietly asserting something untrue.
        versionName = "0.1"

        // There was no instrumentation runner, so there was nowhere to put a
        // test that actually starts the app. That is the gap that let a crash
        // in onCreate ship green: the suite compiled the APK and ran unit
        // tests, and never once launched it.
        testInstrumentationRunner = "androidx.test.runner.AndroidJUnitRunner"
    }

    // The shared debug key, committed at the repository root. Without it AGP mints
    // ~/.android/debug.keystore per machine, and a CI runner is a fresh machine
    // every run - so each build was signed with a different certificate and
    // `adb install -r` over the previous one failed with
    // INSTALL_FAILED_UPDATE_INCOMPATIBLE. The only way through was `adb uninstall`,
    // which wipes the pairing secret, the auth token, the device id and anything
    // still queued offline.
    //
    // Guarded on existence rather than assumed: a checkout of this module alone,
    // without the repository around it, falls back to AGP's generated key and still
    // builds.
    signingConfigs {
        getByName("debug") {
            val shared = rootProject.file("../keystore/debug.keystore")
            if (shared.exists()) {
                storeFile = shared
                storePassword = "android"
                keyAlias = "androiddebugkey"
                keyPassword = "android"
            }
        }
    }

    buildTypes {
        debug { isMinifyEnabled = false }
        release {
            // On, finally. The blocker recorded here for months was "this needs
            // the kotlinx-serialization keep rules" — and the library has
            // shipped them since 1.5: kotlinx-serialization-core 1.7.3, the
            // exact version declared below, carries
            // META-INF/com.android.tools/r8/kotlinx-serialization-r8.pro, and
            // OkHttp 4.12.0 carries META-INF/proguard/okhttp3.pro. R8 consumes
            // both without being asked. Checked by unzipping the artifacts, not
            // by reading about them.
            //
            // This mattered more than a smaller APK, and it is now settled:
            // the workflow publishes THIS build, not the debug one. It gates
            // on an emulator actually installing and starting the shrunk APK
            // before the release is cut, so a shrinker fault cannot ship
            // green. A debuggable build would have ART's optimisations off and
            // every class interpreted — app and libraries alike, including the
            // reactor's frame loop — and would leave `adb shell run-as` open
            // on the data directory, where hardware Keystore binding stops a
            // key being EXTRACTED but not USED by anything running as the app.
            //
            // (This paragraph used to assert the published artifact WAS the
            // debug one. That stopped being true when the release gate landed,
            // and an audit caught the comment still arguing for a decision
            // already made — which would leave a reader thinking the shipped
            // APK is debuggable when it is not.)
            //
            // Signed with the same committed debug key, so `adb install -r`
            // over an existing install still works: the certificate is what
            // has to match, not the build type.
            //
            // THE TRIPWIRE ON THAT KEY, recorded here because this is where
            // someone will be standing when it matters: `keystore/debug.keystore`
            // is committed to this repository. Android decides whether an APK
            // may replace an installed app by CERTIFICATE, and a same-signature
            // update inherits the existing data directory and the Keystore
            // alias — so anyone holding this key can build an app the phone
            // accepts as an update to this one and simply ask the Keystore to
            // decrypt the pairing token. No root, no `run-as`.
            //
            // That is survivable today only because the repository is PRIVATE.
            // It is a one-way door: making the repo public exposes the key
            // retroactively and for every commit in history, and no later
            // rotation can un-publish it. So — rotate this key BEFORE the repo
            // is ever made public or shared, never after. Rotating costs one
            // uninstall/reinstall on the phone and re-pairing, because the new
            // certificate will not match the installed one.
            isMinifyEnabled = true
            isShrinkResources = true
            signingConfig = signingConfigs.getByName("debug")
            proguardFiles(
                getDefaultProguardFile("proguard-android-optimize.txt"),
                "proguard-rules.pro",
            )
        }
    }

    testOptions {
        unitTests {
            // The pattern engine is pure maths over androidx Color, but a stray
            // android.* call in a transitive would otherwise throw "not mocked"
            // rather than returning something the test can assert on.
            isReturnDefaultValues = true
        }
    }

    compileOptions {
        sourceCompatibility = JavaVersion.VERSION_17
        targetCompatibility = JavaVersion.VERSION_17
    }

    buildFeatures {
        compose = true
        buildConfig = true
    }

    packaging {
        resources { excludes += "/META-INF/{AL2.0,LGPL2.1}" }
    }
}

dependencies {
    // NOT the 2026.08.00 the brief pins. That BOM resolves Compose 1.12.0,
    // whose every artifact declares minCompileSdk 37, and CheckAarMetadata
    // enforces that as a hard error. §3 also forbids compiling against 37
    // because it is Beta, so the two requirements cannot both be met.
    //
    // Surveying the BOMs by the minCompileSdk in their own AAR metadata:
    // 1.12.x needs 37, everything from 1.11.3 back to 1.9.1 needs 35. Compose
    // never required 36, so 2026.06.00 is the newest BOM usable here.
    //
    // The brief gives a reason for compileSdk 36 and none for this particular
    // BOM, so the BOM is the constraint that yields.
    implementation(platform("androidx.compose:compose-bom:2026.06.00"))

    implementation("androidx.core:core-ktx:1.15.0")
    // 1.12.4, not 1.9.3. androidx.activity is not managed by the Compose BOM, so
    // that pin was real rather than decorative: resolving the graph in CI shows
    // core-ktx and lifecycle floating up to 1.16.0 and 2.9.4 on their own, while
    // activity stayed at a November 2024 release hosting a 2026 Compose runtime
    // under targetSdk 36 — where edge-to-edge enforcement changed and
    // MainActivity calls enableEdgeToEdge(). 1.12.4 is the last patch of the
    // generation contemporaneous with Compose 1.11.3; 1.13.0 is stable but newer
    // than the BOM, and 1.14.x is alpha.
    implementation("androidx.activity:activity-compose:1.12.4")
    implementation("androidx.lifecycle:lifecycle-runtime-ktx:2.8.7")
    implementation("androidx.lifecycle:lifecycle-runtime-compose:2.8.7")

    implementation("androidx.compose.ui:ui")
    implementation("androidx.compose.ui:ui-graphics")
    implementation("androidx.compose.material3:material3")
    // Used directly (LazyColumn, background, KeyboardOptions) rather than relied on
    // as a material3 transitive.
    implementation("androidx.compose.foundation:foundation")

    // The home-screen approval widget. 1.1.1, not a newer release: it is the
    // exact version the retired jarvis-android/ module already compiled
    // successfully against (compileSdk 35, Compose BOM 2024.10.01) - the only
    // real precedent for this dependency working anywhere in this monorepo,
    // and this project has no local build to verify a different choice
    // against. compileSdk 36 here is a ceiling raised from that module's 35,
    // never lowered, so its own minCompileSdk requirement is still satisfied.
    implementation("androidx.glance:glance-appwidget:1.1.1")
    implementation("androidx.glance:glance:1.1.1")

    // The serialization compiler plugin is applied in build.gradle.kts but no
    // runtime was declared, so the first @Serializable anyone wrote would have
    // failed to resolve rather than working. Step 2's event models need it.
    implementation("org.jetbrains.kotlinx:kotlinx-serialization-json:1.7.3")
    implementation("org.jetbrains.kotlinx:kotlinx-coroutines-android:1.9.0")

    // The transport. OkHttp rather than Ktor because the SSE stream wants a
    // socket held open for an hour with no read timeout, and chat wants a
    // chunked body cancelled mid-flight to interrupt generation - both are
    // one-liners here.
    // A fingerprint instead of a tap for irreversible and outbound decisions -
    // the one item on the brief's list a browser genuinely cannot do.
    implementation("androidx.biometric:biometric:1.1.0")
    // Not used directly - this app has no fragments of its own and is pure
    // Compose. It is here to raise a floor that biometric:1.1.0 sets too low:
    // it depends on androidx.fragment 1.2.5, and `registerForActivityResult`
    // needs 1.3.0 or newer. Gradle takes the highest, so declaring the minimum
    // is all this does.
    //
    // Found by building the release variant for the first time. The check is
    // `InvalidFragmentVersionForActivityResult`, and it is fatal only under
    // lintVitalRelease - which runs on release builds and nothing else - so it
    // had never run at all. That is the whole argument for building this
    // variant in CI: the failure was latent, not new.
    //
    // It is also not merely a lint nag. MainActivity registers two permission
    // contracts at construction, and BiometricPrompt - the gate that rule 4
    // leans on for irreversible approvals - drives a fragment internally.
    implementation("androidx.fragment:fragment:1.3.0")

    implementation("com.squareup.okhttp3:okhttp:4.12.0")
    implementation("com.squareup.okio:okio:3.6.0")

    testImplementation("junit:junit:4.13.2")

    // Deliberately just enough to launch the activity and read its lifecycle
    // state. No Compose test rule: the reactor runs an unbounded
    // withFrameNanos loop, and Compose's test clock treats a running animation
    // as "not idle", so a ComposeTestRule would sit waiting for a face that is
    // never going to stop. ActivityScenario has no such coupling.
    androidTestImplementation("androidx.test.ext:junit:1.2.1")
    androidTestImplementation("androidx.test:core-ktx:1.6.1")
    androidTestImplementation("androidx.test:runner:1.6.2")

    // A server that answers, for the contract tests. Pinned to the same 4.12.0
    // as OkHttp itself: MockWebServer 5.x replaced MockResponse's setters with
    // a builder, so the version is load-bearing, not incidental.
    //
    // androidTest rather than a JVM unit test, deliberately. TokenStore needs
    // the real Keystore and JarvisApi takes it as a constructor argument, so a
    // JVM test could only reach this code by making the token store injectable
    // - a production change to suit a test. On the emulator the whole stack is
    // real, including the network security config, which already permits
    // cleartext to 127.0.0.1.
    androidTestImplementation("com.squareup.okhttp3:mockwebserver:4.12.0")
}
````

## `jarvis-client/app/proguard-rules.pro`

````text
# R8 for the release variant.
#
# Deliberately short, and the reason is worth writing down: nearly everything
# this app needs kept is already kept by rules the libraries ship themselves.
# Verified by looking inside the artifacts rather than by reading a blog:
#
#   kotlinx-serialization-core-jvm-1.7.3.jar
#     META-INF/com.android.tools/r8/kotlinx-serialization-r8.pro
#     META-INF/com.android.tools/r8/kotlinx-serialization-common.pro
#   okhttp-4.12.0.jar
#     META-INF/proguard/okhttp3.pro
#
# R8 consumes those automatically. The build file used to say minification was
# blocked on "the kotlinx-serialization keep rules"; that stopped being true
# several releases ago.
#
# AGP generates keeps for everything named in the manifest - JarvisApp,
# MainActivity, EventService, LinkTileService, BootReceiver - so none of them
# are listed here. The app has no Class.forName, no newInstance, and no
# reflective entry point of its own; every `::class.java` in the source is an
# Intent target or a system-service lookup, both direct references R8 follows.

# The models are reached only through their generated serializers, which the
# library's rules keep. This keeps the @Serializable classes' own members too,
# so a field that is only ever written by the serializer cannot be pruned out
# from under it.
-keepclassmembers,allowobfuscation class com.jarvis.client.net.** {
    *** Companion;
}
-keepclasseswithmembers class com.jarvis.client.net.** {
    kotlinx.serialization.KSerializer serializer(...);
}

# Crash traces are the point of platform/CrashLog: a sideloaded app has no
# Play Console behind it, so the file the owner reads aloud is the only report
# there is. Obfuscated frames would make it useless.
-keepattributes SourceFile,LineNumberTable
-renamesourcefileattribute SourceFile

# WorkManager's own Room database, kept by name.
#
# The paragraph at the top of this file said nothing needed adding beyond
# what the libraries ship themselves. That held until the first real release
# build was actually launched on a device rather than only compiled, and it
# crashed before drawing a frame:
#
#   FATAL EXCEPTION: main
#   Unable to get provider androidx.startup.InitializationProvider
#   Caused by: java.lang.RuntimeException: Failed to create an instance of
#       androidx.work.impl.WorkDatabase
#       at androidx.work.WorkManagerInitializer.b(Unknown Source:93)
#
# Nothing in this app's own source uses WorkManager - it arrives only
# transitively, through androidx.glance:glance-appwidget, which schedules
# widget refreshes through it. WorkManager keeps its own job queue in a Room
# database (androidx.work.impl.WorkDatabase), and Room does not construct
# its generated implementation (WorkDatabase_Impl) directly: it looks the
# class up by name at runtime and reflectively calls its no-arg
# constructor. R8 cannot see that reflective call, so with nothing else in
# the app's static call graph referencing WorkDatabase_Impl, it read the
# constructor as unreachable and stripped it - a known, repeatedly-reported
# failure mode of Room-backed libraries under R8's full mode, not something
# specific to this app. androidx.work's and androidx.room's own consumer
# rules evidently do not cover WorkManager's INTERNAL database this way -
# if they did, the crash above would not have happened - so it is named
# explicitly here instead.
#
# NOT verified by inspecting the library's own bytecode: this container has
# no route to dl.google.com (see the top-level CLAUDE.md), so there is no
# way to open the AAR and check its consumer-rules.pro the way the
# kotlinx-serialization and okhttp entries above were checked. This is the
# documented shape of the exact crash above, matched against the exact
# stack trace this build produced rather than assumed from the library
# name alone - and GitHub Actions, the only compiler this module has, is
# what actually confirms it on the next run.
-keep class * extends androidx.room.RoomDatabase {
    <init>();
}
````

## `jarvis-client/app/src/androidTest/java/com/jarvis/client/ApiContractTest.kt`

````kotlin
package com.jarvis.client

import androidx.test.core.app.ApplicationProvider
import androidx.test.ext.junit.runners.AndroidJUnit4
import com.jarvis.client.data.ClientSettings
import com.jarvis.client.data.TokenStore
import com.jarvis.client.net.ApiError
import com.jarvis.client.net.ApiResult
import com.jarvis.client.net.JarvisApi
import com.jarvis.client.net.SaidAloud
import kotlinx.coroutines.runBlocking
import okhttp3.mockwebserver.Dispatcher
import okhttp3.mockwebserver.MockResponse
import okhttp3.mockwebserver.MockWebServer
import okhttp3.mockwebserver.RecordedRequest
import org.junit.After
import org.junit.Assert.assertEquals
import org.junit.Assert.assertFalse
import org.junit.Assert.assertNotNull
import org.junit.Assert.assertTrue
import org.junit.Before
import org.junit.Test
import org.junit.runner.RunWith
import java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit

/**
 * The client against a server that answers.
 *
 * Everything else in this suite is either a pure-JVM test of one function or a
 * three-assertion check that the app opens. Nothing had ever put the real
 * OkHttp client, the real headers, the real token store and the real decoders
 * end to end against something speaking the protocol — so a mismatch between
 * what this client sends and what `jarvis_hud.py` expects could only be found
 * by a human with a paired phone.
 *
 * MockWebServer rather than a hand-rolled socket: it records what arrived,
 * which is the half that matters. Most of these assertions are about the
 * REQUEST, not the response.
 *
 * On 127.0.0.1, which `network_security_config.xml` already permits in
 * cleartext alongside `localhost` and `ts.net` — so this exercises the real
 * policy rather than working around it.
 */
@RunWith(AndroidJUnit4::class)
class ApiContractTest {

    private val context get() = ApplicationProvider.getApplicationContext<android.content.Context>()

    private lateinit var server: MockWebServer
    private lateinit var settings: ClientSettings
    private lateinit var tokens: TokenStore
    private lateinit var api: JarvisApi

    /** Whatever the dispatcher should answer next, by path. */
    private val routes = mutableMapOf<String, MockResponse>()

    private fun ok(json: String) = MockResponse().setResponseCode(200)
        .setHeader("Content-Type", "application/json").setBody(json)

    @Before
    fun start() {
        server = MockWebServer()
        server.dispatcher = object : Dispatcher() {
            override fun dispatch(request: RecordedRequest): MockResponse {
                val path = request.path?.substringBefore('?').orEmpty()
                return routes[path] ?: MockResponse().setResponseCode(404).setBody("{}")
            }
        }
        server.start()

        settings = ClientSettings(context)
        settings.setHost("${server.hostName}:${server.port}")
        tokens = TokenStore(context)
        tokens.setToken(TOKEN)
        api = JarvisApi(settings, tokens)
    }

    @After
    fun stop() {
        tokens.clear()
        settings.setHost("")
        server.shutdown()
    }

    // ------------------------------------------------------------ headers ---

    /**
     * §2: the token on every request, and `X-Jarvis-Client: hud` beside it.
     *
     * Asserted across several routes rather than one, because the header is
     * applied by a helper that a new endpoint can simply forget to call — and
     * forgetting it is a 403 from the origin check that reads like the desktop
     * being unreachable.
     */
    @Test
    fun everyRequestCarriesTheTokenAndTheClientHeader() = runBlocking {
        routes["/api/status"] = ok("""{"ok":true}""")
        routes["/api/pending"] = ok("""{"available":true,"pending":[]}""")
        routes["/api/attention"] = ok("""{"available":true}""")
        routes["/api/approve"] = ok("{}")

        api.status(); api.pending(); api.attention(); api.approve("a1")

        repeat(4) {
            val req = server.takeRequest(10, TimeUnit.SECONDS)
            assertNotNull("a request never arrived", req)
